HVDC Emerging, with SST in the Pipeline
HVDC coming along with the increasing power consumption
of AI clusters
Why do we need HVDC?
Low voltage turns higher rack power into an extreme-current problem
The fundamental relationship is that at a given rack load, increasing the bulk-distribution
voltage from 54 V to 800 V reduces current by approximately 14.8 times. A balanced
±400 V architecture also presents 800 V between its +400 V and –400 V conductors, so

its bulk-transfer current is broadly equivalent to a 0–800 V architecture.
This current differential has an outsized mechanical consequence. For the same conductor
resistance, I²R loss at 54V would theoretically be approximately 219 times that at 800 V,
though real systems avoid this level of loss by adding copper, parallel conductors and
cooling. The result is not a 219-fold difference in actual operating loss, but a trade-off in
which low-voltage designs consume increasing amounts of busbar cross-section,

connector area, rack volume and cooling capacity to remain within voltage-drop and
temperature limits.
Exhibit 44: Transmission current for different power scales: 54V vs 800V
0
5,000
10,000
15,000
20,000
150KW 250KW 350KW 450KW 600KW 1000KW
Ampere
54V 800V
Source: Delta
Copper, connectors and thermal management become system constraints
At several thousand amperes, distribution cannot rely on a single ordinary conductor or
connector. Designers must use broad laminated busbars, multiple parallel contacts, tightly

controlled joint resistance and temperature monitoring. A small increase in contact
resistance produces disproportionately high heat at these currents, raising the risk of hot
spots, connector ageing and nuisance protection events. OCP’s 100KW HVDC-to-LVDC
shelf illustrates where the current problem moves: only two 62.5 A HVDC inputs are
required on the ±400V side, while the 49–50V output approaches 2,000A and therefore
uses a substantial bolted output connection with dedicated thermistors.

### Page 40

M
Asia Pacific Insight
40
The same issue appears in power-shelf packaging. Delta notes that a typical AI rack may
already allocate roughly three-quarters of its volume to servers, leaving insufficient room
for additional power shelves, BBUs or supercapacitor shelves as power rises. Moving AC/
DC conversion, protection and storage into an adjacent power rack therefore releases
compute space and separates the thermal design of power electronics from that of GPUs.

Microsoft’s Mt Diablo rationale similarly states that disaggregated power can enable up to
35% more accelerators in the IT rack and allows power shelf count to be sized
independently for each workload configuration
Exhibit 45: Not enough room for power devices on the traditional AI server rack
Source: Delta
HVDC enables denser, fewer and more efficient power stages
As AI rack power scales from today's  GB200/GB300 systems to Vera Rubin and future

multi-hundred-kilowatt racks, power distribution efficiency becomes increasingly
important. Our checks indicate that a conventional 480VAC architecture delivers an end-
to-end electrical efficiency of roughly 93%, while an 800VDC architecture improves
efficiency to about 95% by eliminating one power conversion stage and simplifying the
power path from grid to rack.
The higher-voltage 800VDC distribution also reduces current levels for the same power

load, lowering resistive (I²R) losses in cables and distribution equipment and enabling
more efficient delivery to increasingly power-dense AI racks.
While a 2ppts of efficiency improvement may appear modest, the savings become
substantial at hyperscale AI data center deployments. Delta's example shows that for a
100MW IT load, improving facility efficiency from 93% to 95% reduces annual energy
consumption by approximately 19,800MWh, translating into roughly US$1.6 million of

annual electricity cost savings assuming a power price of US$0.08/kWh. At a 1GW AI
campus, the savings scale to nearly US$16 million per year, and at emerging multi-
gigawatt AI factory deployments, the annual benefit can exceed US$100 million.
Consequently, the transition from conventional AC distribution and 54VDC rack power
architectures toward 800VDC distribution is not only a technical necessity for supporting
future 500kW-to-1MW AI racks, but also a compelling economic proposition that lowers

operating expenses and improves total cost of ownership through reduced energy losses
and simplified electrical infrastructure.

### Page 41

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 41
Exhibit 46: Better power efficiency for 800VDC architecture
Source: Delta
Exhibit 47: Better efficiency leads to cost savings
Source: Delta
What's the deployment roadmap for HVDC?
The deployment roadmap for 800VDC is best understood as a multi-stage evolution
rather than a single-step transition. Both Nvidia and major hyperscalers recognize that
existing 480VAC infrastructure cannot efficiently support the rapid increase in rack

power. As rack densities increase, conventional power whips, busbars, connectors, and
PSU architectures increasingly become bottlenecks, driving the need for higher voltage
distribution
The first phase of deployment focuses on introducing 800VDC power racks within
existing AC-based data centers. In this architecture, the facility remains largely unchanged,
but a dedicated power rack converts 415-480VAC into 800VDC before distributing power
locally to AI racks. This approach minimizes disruption to existing infrastructure while

allowing operators to deploy Vera Rubin-class systems that exceed the practical limits of
traditional power shelves. Nvidia refers to this as the row-level power rack architecture,
which serves as the industry's bridge solution between legacy AC facilities and future
native DC environments.
Exhibit 48: Phase I - 800VDC power rack
Source: Nvidia

### Page 42

M
Asia Pacific Insight
42
The second phase expands 800VDC beyond individual rows into data hall-level power
centers. Instead of deploying a dedicated power rack for every few compute cabinets,
centralized rectifier or power-center systems distribute 800VDC through DC busways
across an entire hall. Nvidia's roadmap identifies 1.6-2MW power centers and 1250A DC
busway architectures as the next step, improving power density while reducing equipment
duplication. This stage also aligns well with the deployment strategies being discussed by

hyperscalers and the OCP community because it preserves existing medium-voltage and
low-voltage infrastructure while moving a significant portion of distribution into DC
Exhibit 49: Phase II –  800VDC power center
Source: Nvidia
The third phase is the transition toward facility-wide 800VDC distribution, where AC is
converted to DC near the utility entrance or medium-voltage substation and distributed
throughout the data center via high-capacity DC switchboards and busways. Nvidia

describes this as the DC power block architecture, utilizing approximately 4.8MW power
blocks, 6000A DC busways, and integrated redundancy schemes. At this stage, the
architecture begins to resemble an "AI factory," where power distribution is simplified,
energy storage can be integrated directly onto the DC bus, and multiple megawatts of
compute capacity can be deployed as modular building blocks

### Page 43

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 43
Exhibit 50: Phase III –  power block
Source: Nvidia
The final stage is the emergence of native 800VDC compute racks, which Nvidia identifies
as the long-term destination. Today, even in 800VDC deployments, power is generally
stepped down to 54V before entering the servers. Future systems, however, are expected
to accept 800VDC directly within the rack and potentially at the server level, eliminating

another conversion stage and further reducing power losses. Nvidia's public roadmap
shows a progression from current 54V architectures, to 800VDC-fed PSUs, and ultimately
to 1MW-class native 800VDC racks capable of supporting next-generation AI accelerators
Exhibit 51: 800VDC native server racks
Source: Nvidia
In sum, deployment is likely to occur gradually because the ecosystem remains under
development. NVIDIA notes that several enabling technologies—including DC protection

devices, busways, connectors, grounding standards, arc-flash modeling, and solid-state
transformers— are still undergoing certification and standardization. The roadmap
presented by NVIDIA suggests a phased commercialization timeline, with 800VDC power
racks reaching deployment readiness in 2026, hall-level power centers in 2027, and
facility-scale DC power blocks becoming deployment-ready around 2028. Industry

### Page 44

M
Asia Pacific Insight
44
collaboration among NVIDIA, Microsoft, Google, and OCP participants is focused on
ensuring interoperability and regulatory acceptance before broader hyperscale adoption.
Exhibit 52: HVDC deployment roadmap
Source: Nvidia
800VDC represents 4-5x dollar content vs. in-rack power supply
architecture
The 800VDC power rack BOM is primarily driven by the PSU, which accounts for 50–55%
of the total cost, followed by the BBU at 25–30%, with other components comprising the

remaining 15–20%. This cost structure reflects the broader functionality integrated into an
HVDC power rack, combining power conversion, backup power and supporting
components within a centralized architecture.
For power supply players, one of the most implications is that an HVDC power rack carries
around 4–5x higher dollar content per watt compared with a conventional in-rack PSU
solution, since the former integrates more components into a robust system and power

supply players usually ship the entire rack instead of just the components.

800VDC vs. ±400VDC?
The debate between ±400VDC and 800VDC centers on balancing near-term practicality
against long-term scalability. While both architectures deliver an identical 800V
differential voltage and therefore achieve similar reductions in current, copper loss, and
cable size versus traditional 54V rack power systems, they differ significantly in
implementation.
Under a ±400VDC architecture, each conductor operates at only 400V relative to ground,

resulting in less stringent insulation, creepage, clearance, and protection requirements.
This makes the architecture easier to deploy, maintain, and certify, which explains why
many hyperscalers and OCP members view ±400VDC as an attractive migration path
toward high-density AI racks.
By contrast, NVIDIA and several power infrastructure vendors are increasingly advocating
for a single-bus 800VDC architecture because it simplifies overall power distribution,

reduces conversion stages, and is better suited for future 500kW to 1MW rack
deployments.
We believe that ±400VDC appears well positioned as a near-term bridge architecture due
to its lower deployment risk and more mature ecosystem, whereas 800VDC is
increasingly viewed as the long-term end-state architecture for AI factories as rack power
scales toward the megawatt era.
Exhibit 55: Comparison of 800VDC and ±400VDC
Metric ±400VDC 800VDC
Voltage to ground 400V relative to ground 800V relative to ground

Voltage differential 800V 800V
Power distribution efficiency High Slightly higher
Scalability Good Excellent
Insulation requirements Lower Higher
Maintenance safety Better More challenging

Source: Morgan Stanley Research
From a component supplier perspective, the transition toward HVDC is far more
important than which architecture ultimately wins, as most hardware content increases
under either scenario. PSU vendors, BBU suppliers, busbar manufacturers, connector
providers, power-whip suppliers, magnetics vendors, and rack-level DC-DC converter

manufacturers are largely architecture-agnostic, since both ±400VDC and 800VDC
require a similar shift toward high-voltage power distribution, centralized power shelves,
and rack-level conversion.
Our understanding is that the primary exception is the power semiconductor industry,
where the choice of architecture could materially influence device content and technology
adoption. A ±400VDC system can often be supported using existing 650V-900V MOSFET

and SiC platforms, benefiting suppliers with exposure to lower-voltage power devices. By
contrast, 800VDC architectures increasingly require 1200V-class SiC MOSFETs, higher-

### Page 46

M
Asia Pacific Insight
46
voltage gate drivers, hot-swap controllers, isolation components, and advanced protection
circuitry, driving greater semiconductor content per watt and raising technology barriers.
Solid state transformer potentially being the holy grail in AI
datacenter power delivery
What is a solid-state transformer ?
A datacenter solid state transformer (SST) integrates power electronics to replace or
augment traditional line-frequency transformers, and it's typically built around three

major functional stages. The first is the medium-voltage AC-to-DC rectifier stage, which
uses high-voltage semiconductor switches—often silicon carbide (SiC) or gallium nitride
(GaN) devices due to their superior switching speed and thermal performance—to convert
incoming grid-level medium voltage AC into a regulated DC bus. The second is the isolated
DC-DC conversion stage, usually implemented as a dual active bridge (DAB) or LLC
resonant converter, which provides galvanic isolation through a high-frequency

transformer (operating in the kHz to tens-of-kHz range rather than 50/60 Hz),
dramatically shrinking the magnetic core and transformer size compared to conventional
units while stepping voltage down toward datacenter distribution levels. The third is the
low-voltage DC-AC or DC-DC output stage, which conditions power into the form needed
by the facility—either regulated DC for direct DC-bus distribution to IT racks or inverted
AC for legacy PDU/UPS infrastructure. Supporting these power stages are the control and

sensing subsystems (digital controllers, gate drivers, voltage/current sensors) that
manage switching, fault protection, and grid synchronization; thermal management
components (heat sinks, cold plates, or liquid cooling loops) to handle the concentrated
losses in the semiconductor devices; and increasingly, integrated energy storage interfaces
or DC microgrid ports that allow the SST to natively support battery buffering, renewable
integration, and finer-grained power quality control than a passive transformer ever could

A Solid-State Transformer (SST) is a power-electronics-based transformer that replaces
the traditional 50/60Hz iron-core transformer with a combination of power
semiconductors, control electronics, and a medium-frequency transformer. Unlike
conventional transformers that perform only voltage conversion, SSTs actively process
power through multiple stages, enabling both AC and DC inputs/outputs, bidirectional
power flow, voltage regulation, reactive power compensation, and intelligent grid control

A SST is  typically built around three major functional stages:
• The first is the medium-voltage AC-to-DC rectifier stage, which uses high-voltage
semiconductor switches—often silicon carbide (SiC) or gallium nitride (GaN)
devices due to their superior switching speed and thermal performance—to
convert incoming grid-level medium voltage AC into a regulated DC bus.
• The second is the isolated DC-DC conversion stage, usually implemented as a dual

active bridge (DAB) or LLC resonant converter, which provides galvanic isolation
through a high-frequency transformer (operating in the KHz to tens-of-LHz range
rather than 50/60 Hz), dramatically shrinking the magnetic core and transformer
size compared to conventional units while stepping voltage down toward
datacenter distribution levels.
• The third is the low-voltage DC-AC or DC-DC output stage, which conditions

### Page 47

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 47
power into the form needed by the facility—either regulated DC for direct DC-bus
distribution to IT racks or inverted AC for legacy PDU/UPS infrastructure.
Supporting these power stages are the control and sensing subsystems (digital
controllers, gate drivers, voltage/current sensors) that manage switching, fault protection,
and grid synchronization; thermal management components (heat sinks, cold plates, or

liquid cooling loops) to handle the concentrated losses in the semiconductor devices; and
increasingly, integrated energy storage interfaces or DC microgrid ports that allow the SST
to natively support battery buffering, renewable integration, and finer-grained power
quality control than a passive transformer ever could.
Exhibit 56: Solid state vs. traditional transformers
Source: Infineon
Why do we need SSTs?
Improving efficiency in an era of gigawatt data centers

One of the strongest drivers for SST adoption is the rapid increase in AI data center power
demand, which  has progressed from 5-10MW facilities in the pre-AI era to campuses
exceeding 650MW today, with gigawatt-scale facilities expected beyond 2029.
Traditional architectures use medium-voltage AC distribution, followed by low-frequency
transformers and multiple power conversion stages before power reaches IT racks. SSTs
allow facilities to convert 10-34.5KV medium-voltage power directly into ±400V or 800V

DC distribution networks. This reduces conversion losses and improves overall grid-to-
chip efficiency.
The efficiency advantage could be at the order of about 2-5ppt vs, the traditional
transformer architecture, but at a 1GW AI campus, this magnitude of improvement could
translate into roughly 20-50MW of continuous power savings. Over the lifetime of a
facility, this can result in substantial reductions in electricity cost and carbon emissions.
SSTs are therefore becoming increasingly attractive for hyperscaler operators seeking

lower total cost of ownership (TCO).

### Page 48

M
Asia Pacific Insight
48
Exhibit 57: SST enabling more efficient power delivery architecture
Source: Infineon
Higher power density and smaller footprint
Because SSTs use medium-frequency transformers instead of bulky 50/60Hz
transformers, they can achieve significantly higher power density. Infineon indicates that
SST systems can reduce both footprint and weight by at least 30% compared with
conventional transformer-based installations. They also accelerate site energization and

facilitate modular deployment
This becomes particularly valuable in urban data centers, utility substations, EV charging
hubs, and energy storage sites where space constraints directly affect project economics.
Exhibit 58: SST offers better power efficiency with at least 30% less space/weight

Supporting distributed energy resources
As renewable energy penetration increases, future grids will require seamless integration
of solar generation, battery storage, fuel cells, gas turbines, and other distributed energy
resources.
SSTs provide intelligent power routing, bidirectional operation, and dynamic voltage
control. This allows them to function as energy hubs that connect renewable generation,

### Page 49

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 49
storage, and loads through a common DC infrastructure. Such capabilities are difficult or
impossible to achieve using passive transformers alone.
Improved grid intelligence
Unlike conventional transformers, SSTs can actively regulate voltage and frequency,
provide reactive power compensation, support grid-forming functionality, and improve
overall power quality. These capabilities make SSTs an important enabler for future smart

grids, microgrids, and virtual power plants.
What's the deployment status now?
Despite decades of research, SSTs remain at an early commercial deployment stage. While
we started to see the SST models being unveiled by relevant suppliers and there start to
be some pilot adoption, we believe that the 800VDC power rack/center/block
architecture shall be deployed first, and SSTs will take more time, as more fundamental
design change in the datacenter and all hardware inside will involved. We believe wider

adoption probably won't happen before 2030.
Exhibit 59: SST from Delta
Source: Delta
Exhibit 60: SST from Eaton
Source: Eaton
According to Infineon, SST could represent a >US$1bn market opportunity by 2030, as the
technology begins to replace a portion of the >US$15bn conventional transformer market,
particularly in smaller-power applications. While SST penetration is expected to remain
relatively modest versus the overall transformer market, the opportunity is significant for

relevant players in the longer term.

50
Exhibit 61: SST to see initial adoption in 2030
Source: Infineon
Key hurdles for the wider adoption of SSTs
• High system cost remains the primary barrier: Despite their technical advantages,
SSTs remain significantly more expensive than conventional transformers.
Traditional transformers consist primarily of steel cores, copper windings,
insulation materials, and cooling systems, all of which benefit from mature
manufacturing processes and economies of scale. In contrast, SSTs require large

numbers of high-voltage power semiconductors, gate drivers, digital controllers,
sensors, protection systems, and sophisticated thermal management solutions. The
extensive use of silicon carbide (SiC) devices, which remain substantially more
expensive than conventional silicon-based components, further increases the
overall system cost. Although SSTs can deliver efficiency gains, improved
controllability, and reduced footprint, the economic benefit is often insufficient to

offset the substantial upfront capital expenditure in many utility applications.
Consequently, SST deployment is currently limited to applications where
efficiency, power density, or system flexibility carry exceptionally high economic
value, such as AI data centers, megawatt EV charging stations, and grid-scale
battery storage systems.
• Long term reliability has yet to be proven: Electric utilities and large
infrastructure operators place an extremely high value on reliability because

transformers are expected to operate continuously for several decades with
minimal maintenance. Conventional transformers routinely achieve service
lifetimes exceeding 30 to 40 years and have accumulated more than a century of
operating history. SSTs, however, are fundamentally different devices that
incorporate thousands of semiconductor junctions, complex control electronics,
communication networks, cooling systems, and software algorithms. While each

individual technology may be mature, the long-term reliability of the complete SST
system remains largely unproven at scale. Operators therefore remain cautious
about replacing a passive asset with an active power-electronic system whose
failure modes are more complex and less understood. For critical infrastructure
such as substations, industrial facilities, or hyperscale data centers, reliability
concerns often outweigh potential efficiency improvements.

### Page 51

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 51
• DC protection technology ss still evolving: One of the most significant technical
challenges associated with SST deployment is fault protection, particularly in
medium-voltage and high-voltage DC networks. Unlike AC systems, where current
naturally crosses zero multiple times each cycle and facilitates fault interruption,
DC currents continue flowing continuously during fault conditions. As a result,

fault currents can rise extremely quickly, making them more difficult to detect and
interrupt safely. Future SST-based power architectures therefore require advanced
protection devices such as solid-state circuit breakers (SSCBs), which can react far
faster than conventional mechanical breakers. However, DC protection
technologies are still evolving, and there is currently no universally accepted
approach equivalent to the mature protection schemes available in AC grids. The

absence of standardized protection architectures increases deployment risk and
remains one of the key obstacles to the wider adoption of SST-based DC
distribution systems.
• System complexity creates engineering, qualification and maintenance
challenges: Compared with traditional transformers, SSTs are considerably more
complex systems that require the integration of multiple high-performance
subsystems. A complete SST includes medium-voltage power converters, isolated

DC/DC stages, high-frequency transformers, semiconductor modules, digital
controllers, communication interfaces, and advanced protection mechanisms. As
voltage and power levels increase, the number of converter modules and
semiconductor devices required can rise significantly. Although the introduction of
higher-voltage SiC devices can reduce subsystem count and simplify design, SST
architectures remain substantially more complicated than conventional

transformers. This complexity increases development costs, certification
requirements, maintenance needs, and operational risks, all of which slow adoption
among traditionally conservative utility customers.
• Industry standards and ecosystems are still developing: The transformer
industry benefits from decades of established standards, testing procedures,
maintenance practices, and supply chain infrastructure. SSTs, by comparison,
represent an emerging technology with limited deployment history and evolving

standards. Many utilities remain uncertain regarding qualification procedures,
maintenance requirements, spare-parts strategies, and long-term asset
management. Furthermore, widespread SST adoption will require parallel
development of supporting technologies such as DC protection systems, DC
switchgear, energy management software, and medium-voltage DC distribution
networks. Until the broader ecosystem matures, many customers will continue to
view SSTs as a promising but relatively high-risk alternative to proven transformer

technologies.

### Page 52

M
Asia Pacific Insight
52
Fuel Cells for Better Flexibility and Resilience
What are fuel cells?
Fuel cells are electrochemical energy-conversion devices that generate electricity by
combining hydrogen and oxygen without combustion. Unlike conventional power
generation technologies, which convert fuel into heat and then into electricity through
multiple intermediate steps, fuel cells produce electricity directly through an
electrochemical reaction, resulting in higher efficiency and significantly lower emissions.

When powered by hydrogen, the primary byproducts are water and heat, making fuel cells
one of the cleanest forms of power generation available today.
Fuel cells can be deployed across a wide range of applications, including passenger
vehicles, heavy-duty trucks, buses, trains, ships, stationary power generation, backup
power systems, and increasingly data centers. As governments and industries pursue
decarbonization while seeking reliable and dispatchable power sources.

Major fuel-cell technologies include:
• SOFC (Solid Oxide Fuel Cell): Preferred for stationary power and data centers.
• PEMFC (Proton Exchange Membrane Fuel Cell): Dominant in transportation and
backup power.
• MCFC (Molten Carbonate Fuel Cell): Large-scale distributed generation.
• PAFC (Phosphoric Acid Fuel Cell): Commercial combined heat and power.
• DMFC (Direct Methanol Fuel Cell): Portable applications
Exhibit 62: Fuel cell offering - Delta

Source: Delta
Exhibit 63: Fuel cell offering - Bloom Energy
Source: Bloom Energy
How do fuel cells work?
While there are several fuel-cell architectures, the fundamental operating principle is
largely the same: fuel is supplied to the anode, oxygen is supplied to the cathode, and an
electrolyte in between allows charged ions to move while forcing electrons to flow
through an external circuit. This flow of electrons generates usable electricity, while the

### Page 53

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 53
reaction also produces heat and water as byproducts. Unlike conventional generators,
which rely on burning fuel to drive a turbine or engine, fuel cells produce electricity
directly through electrochemical reactions, allowing them to achieve significantly higher
efficiency and lower emissions.
Using solid oxide fuel cell (SOFC) technology as an example, air is supplied to the cathode
side of the cell, where oxygen molecules gain electrons and form oxygen ions (O²⁻). These

oxygen ions migrate through a ceramic electrolyte toward the anode side of the cell. At
the same time, hydrogen or another hydrogen-rich fuel is supplied to the anode. When the
oxygen ions reach the anode, they react with the fuel to produce water, heat, and
electrons. The generated electrons cannot pass through the electrolyte and therefore
travel through an external electrical circuit before returning to the cathode, creating a
continuous flow of electricity. As long as fuel and air continue to be supplied, the fuel cell

can generate power continuously.
Exhibit 64: Fuel cell diagram
Source: 3M
What benefits can fuel cells offer?
• High energy efficiency: Fuel cells convert the chemical energy of hydrogen
directly into electricity through an electrochemical reaction, avoiding the multiple
energy-conversion steps associated with combustion-based power generation. This
allows fuel cells to achieve electrical efficiencies of over 60%, with some systems
exceeding 80% total efficiency when waste heat is recovered through combined

heat-and-power configurations, compared with roughly 35-45% for conventional
gas-fired power generation. The efficiency advantage translates directly into lower
fuel consumption, reduced operating costs, and improved energy utilization over
the lifetime of the system.
• Low carbon emissions: When powered by hydrogen, fuel cells produce only water
and heat as byproducts, generating no direct carbon emissions at the point of use.
Even when operating on hydrogen derived from natural gas, fuel cells typically

produce significantly lower greenhouse gas emissions than conventional
combustion-based power systems. As green hydrogen production scales, fuel cells
offer a potential pathway toward near-zero-carbon power generation for
transportation, industrial facilities, and distributed energy systems.

### Page 54

M
Asia Pacific Insight
54
• Grid independence and energy security: Fuel cells can provide reliable on-site
power generation independent of the electrical grid, improving energy security and
resilience. This makes them attractive for mission-critical applications such as data
centers, hospitals, telecommunications infrastructure, defense installations, and
industrial facilities that require uninterrupted power. In regions facing grid
congestion or reliability concerns, fuel cells can also serve as a distributed power

source located directly at the point of consumption.
• Long duration energy storage enabler: Fuel cells play an important role in the
emerging hydrogen economy by enabling excess renewable energy from solar and
wind farms to be stored as hydrogen and later converted back into electricity.
Unlike batteries, which become increasingly expensive for longer storage durations,
hydrogen storage can economically provide energy over days, weeks, or even
seasonal periods. This capability could become increasingly valuable as renewable

energy penetration rises worldwide.
• Fuel flexibility: Certain fuel-cell technologies, particularly Solid Oxide Fuel Cells
(SOFCs), can operate on a variety of fuels including hydrogen, natural gas, biogas,
ammonia-derived hydrogen, and other hydrogen-rich fuels. This flexibility allows
users to deploy fuel-cell systems today using existing fuel infrastructure while
maintaining a pathway toward pure hydrogen operation as hydrogen supply chains
mature.

• Quiet operation and reduced maintenance: Because fuel cells contain relatively
few moving parts compared with combustion engines and turbine-based
generators, they operate quietly and often require less routine maintenance. The
absence of combustion also reduces mechanical wear and vibration, which can
improve system reliability and lower lifecycle operating costs, particularly in
stationary power applications.
• Scalability across multiple applications: Fuel cells can be deployed across a wide

range of power levels, from portable power systems measured in watts to utility-
scale installations generating multiple megawatts. This scalability allows the
technology to serve residential buildings, commercial facilities, industrial plants,
transportation platforms, microgrids, and large-scale data centers, making fuel
cells one of the most versatile clean-energy technologies available today.
Limitations and constraints of fuel cells
• High system cost: Despite significant technological progress, fuel cells remain

more expensive than many conventional power-generation technologies. The cost
challenge stems from complex stack manufacturing, specialized materials, balance-
of-plant components, and relatively low production volumes compared with
mature technologies such as internal combustion engines, gas turbines, and
batteries. While costs have declined over the past decade, achieving widespread
commercial adoption will likely require further manufacturing scale, supply-chain

development, and technological improvements.
• Limited hydrogen infrastructure: One of the biggest challenges facing fuel-cell
deployment is the lack of hydrogen production, transportation, storage, and
refueling infrastructure. Unlike electricity, which is already widely distributed
through established grids, hydrogen supply networks remain in the early stages of

### Page 55

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 55
development in most regions. This infrastructure gap creates a chicken-and-egg
challenge, where end users are reluctant to adopt fuel cell technologies without
reliable hydrogen availability, while infrastructure developers are hesitant to invest
until demand reaches sufficient scale.
• Durability and lifetime requirements: Although fuel-cell durability has improved
substantially, long-term reliability remains a key focus area, particularly for

commercial transportation and utility-scale installations. Continuous exposure to
heat, humidity, contaminants, and repeated operating cycles can gradually degrade
fuel cell performance over time. Customers in sectors such as transportation,
power generation, and industrial infrastructure often require operating lifetimes
measured in tens of thousands of hours, creating significant engineering challenges
for system developers.
• Regulatory and safety considerations: Hydrogen is a highly flammable gas and

requires specialized handling, storage, and transportation systems. Although
hydrogen technologies have well-established safety standards, new fueling
stations, pipelines, and storage facilities must comply with stringent regulations.
Developing these standards and obtaining approvals can extend deployment
timelines and increase project costs.
• Technology fragmentation and lack of standardization: The fuel cell industry
encompasses multiple technology platforms, including PEM, SOFC, MCFC, and

other architectures, each optimized for different applications. While this diversity
encourages innovation, it can also slow industry standardization and create
uncertainty for customers evaluating long-term technology choices. Unlike the
battery industry, which is increasingly converging around a few dominant
chemistries, the fuel cell sector remains relatively fragmented.
Implications to datacenter power architecture
Transition from backup power to primary power source

Traditionally, data centers rely on grid electricity for primary power and diesel generators
for backup power during outages. Fuel cells are increasingly being evaluated not only as a
replacement for diesel backup generators but also as a potential primary power source. As
AI-driven workloads substantially increase power density and overall electricity demand,
hyperscalers are exploring fuel cells as a means of deploying computing capacity in
locations where grid power is constrained or unavailable. This could fundamentally shift

data-center power architecture from a grid-centric design to one that incorporates
distributed on-site generation.
Potential long term evolution toward microgrid-based datacenters
In the longer term, fuel cells could become a core component of data-center microgrids
combining renewable generation, battery storage, hydrogen storage, and intelligent
energy management systems. Rather than functioning as passive consumers of grid
electricity, future AI-focused data centers may increasingly operate as self-contained

energy ecosystems capable of managing their own generation, storage, and load balancing.
Such an architecture could provide greater scalability, resilience, and energy independence
than traditional grid-connected designs

### Page 56

M
Asia Pacific Insight
56
Potential simplification of power distribution architecture
Fuel cells inherently generate DC power, which aligns more closely with the power
requirements of modern servers and IT equipment. Current data centers typically involve
multiple AC/DC and DC/DC conversion stages between the utility grid and the processor,
each introducing efficiency losses. Over time, fuel cell adoption could encourage a greater
migration toward direct DC power distribution architectures, reducing conversion losses

and improving overall facility efficiency. Moreover, its DC output fits well with the shift
into HVDC power delivery architecture for datacenters.
Exhibit 65: Fuel cells in datacenter power delivery architecture
Source: Delta

Valuation Methodology –  Delta
Our price target (base case scenario value) is NT$2,500, derived from 30x 2027-28e
P/E: We use price/earnings as our valuation methodology because we believe it
appropriately captures Delta's earnings growth potential. We believe our target P/E
multiple of 30x is justified by an earnings CAGR of 63% over 2025-28, which implies a
PEG of 0.5. This is lower than the average of 1.0 in 2022-25, when the stock was trading at

an average P/E of 23x for a 23% earnings CAGR.
Bull case scenario value – NT$3,300: We apply a 40x P/E multiple  to our average base
case estimate for  2027-2028. This scenario assumes a greater-than-expected contribution
from higher-margin businesses such as power electronics and liquid cooling as well as
greater synergies from acquired companies.
Bear case scenario value – NT$1,150:  We apply a 14x P/E multiple to our average base
case estimate for  2027-2028. This scenario assumes further profit drag from legacy

products, smaller profit contribution from higher-margin businesses, higher opex and/or
less meaningful contribution from acquired companies.

62
Why Does Delta Trade at a Significant Valuation Discount to
Delta Thailand?
Discussion of the valuation gap between Delta Electronics (2308.TW, currently 33x 12M
forward consensus P/E) and Delta Electronics Thailand (DEL TA.TH, 71x currently) has been
ongoing for several years. If we look at their valuations before 2020, Delta was actually
trading at a premium, with an average 17x 12M forward P/E in 2010-2020 vs. Delta
Thailand's 13x. This made sense, as Delta is much more competitive in the global arena and

Delta Thailand was set up as a subsidiary focused on manufacturing.
The situation flipped after 2020. Though Delta has been re-rated to trade at an average
23x 12M forward P/E since 2020, Delta Thailand has seen its valuation surge to an average
of 60x over the same period. This cannot be explained by moves in their respective
markets, either, as TWSE went from 14x forward P/E in 2010-20 to 16x after 2020, while
the SET went from 13x to 16x.
We think this could be attributable to a few factors:

• Different positioning within the Delta group: Delta group has increased its stake
in Delta Thailand to over 60% from about 20% in 2019 through a tender offer.
Since then, there has been much more collaboration between Delta group and
Delta Thailand. As Thailand emerged as one of the major manufacturing hubs
within the Delta group, its earnings growth (28% in 2020-25) outpaced that of
Delta Taiwan (19%).
• Scarcity in the Thai stock market: Delta Thailand is one of the relatively few

large-cap Thai-listed companies that offer   direct exposure to AI/data-center power
and EV-related themes, whereas there are many such alternatives in Taiwan.
• Limited free float: As the Delta group owns a ~62% stake in Delta Thailand and
there have been a few quite stable long-term shareholders, we think the effective
free float is probably 20% or less.

Global Footprint –  Delta
Comprehensive Global Operations
Aggressive capacity expansion plans
The company has been continuously diversifying its production base away from China
owing to the trade turmoil back in 2018-2019, and then by supply chain disruptions during
Covid. Management explicitly set a target of lowering China production to 60% of total
capacity (from >70% previously) for the first time back in 2020. New capacity was added

in Thailand, India, and Taiwan during 2020-2021. It then saw demand surge from various
sectors, including EVs, automation and telecom, during 2022-23, followed by AI-related
power suppliers and cooling solutions emerging in 2024. In order to grasp the emerging
opportunities, relevant plant build-outs were planned accordingly, on top of the ongoing
geographical diversification.  It expanded its presence—production sites, R&D, and sales
teams—in Europe and the US to better cope with operational disruptions and capture

domestic demand, in addition to its existing setups in Taiwan, Thailand, and India. There
was then a substantial increase in capex of NT$12.2bn in 2025 (vs. NT$5.6-6bn in 2023-24)
as the company further accelerated its expansion plan, mainly on AI-related power/cooling
demand and increasing capacity tightness, specifically in Thailand and the US. Delta
continued to expand in Thailand by adding three new plants, with another three under
construction for power and liquid cooling system offerings. In view of the  capacity

tightness reserved for AI-related business, it also started to prioritize margin-accretive
business—i.e., de-emphasize business with net margins below 10%—which further
optimized product mix.
Looking ahead into 2026 and beyond, expansion is likely to remain aggressive in order to
fulfill substantial power and liquid cooling turnkey solution demand for large-scale data
centers, while management also indicated that the new capacity added at the end of 2025

has already been fully taken. With capacity staying tight for AI power and cooling
offerings, the company will continue to expand actively in China, Thailand, the US, and
Tainan, Taiwan. It also plans to add mass production lines in Taoyuan, scheduled to be
ready by 2028, in addition to the pilot run lines in Tainan. Against this backdrop, the capex
budget has been revised up several times and is now roughly NT$50bn for 2026.

Financial Analysis –  Delta
44% Revenue CAGR in 2025-28e
Delta's revenue growth was moderate in 2020 at merely 5%, making it the only year of
the Covid disruption (2019-22) that did not see double-digit growth. Growth picked up to
11% in 2021, underpinned by surging demand for cloud, networking, and work-from-home
infrastructure amid the pandemic, while the next uplift to 22% growth in 2022 was mainly
fueled by increasing demand for EV- and data center-related components (server power

supplies, networking switches, energy storage systems, etc). Revenue momentum
subsequently moderated in 2023, growing just 4%, as the EV component business was
impacted by the UAW strike in the US and automation demand in China stayed subdued,
despite sustained demand  for AI server power supplies and modules. Growth in 2024 was
at a similar level, as strength in data center offerings were not enough to offset weakness
in its legacy businesses.
Revenue surged 32% in 2025 to a record NT$554,885mn, driven by AI server rack power

and cooling solutions, along with further spec upgrades and value growth.
We expect revenue to expand at a 44% CAGR over 2025-28, supported by ongoing AI
server shipments, spec upgrades, 800 VDC power solutions, and turnkey liquid cooling
offerings. As the upgrade  to 800V HVDC power architecture is likely to take years given
the complex design and high safety requirements, we expect AI server power solution
upgrades to be separated into a few stages – from power shelves in AI server racks to

standalone side-car power racks, from 50V DC to 800V DC output, from 72kW per power
shelf to >250kW, etc, as well as power wattage increases for next-generation GPU rack
designs.
By segment, Power Electronics has been the primary revenue driver, contributing at least
50% over the past years despite the company separating the Mobility segment from
Power Electronics since 2024 so as to further distinguish revenue generated from EVs/
autos. We expect the segment to remain the key revenue contributor, supported by strong

demand from AI server-related power solutions, and estimate it will reach 58% of revenue
by 2028 vs. 50% in 2025. Infrastructure, the second-largest revenue contributor
historically, is expected to grow along with the Power Electronics segment, fueled by
demand for data center power-related solutions (i.e. UPSs, CDUs, sidecars, etc) as well as
energy infrastructure-related offerings (i.e. SSTs (solid-state transformers), fuel cells,
modular power solutions, micro grid management, etc.).  We model the segment's revenue

contribution to reach 35% by 2028 from 24% in 2024 and 33% in 2025. For  Automation
and Mobility, we expect their revenue contributions to decline sequentially throughout
the years, with Automation dropping from 10% in 2025 to 4% in 2028 and Mobility falling
from 7% in 2025 to 2% in 2028.

Continuous margin expansion on favorable product mix
Delta's gross margin stayed largely between 28-29% during 2021-23, as revenue was
mainly driven by relatively lower-margin products, including EV- and consumer/IT-related

products, while server-related offerings had yet to scale up. 2024 served as an inflection
point, with the product mix changing structurally, supported by robust demand for AI data
center-related offerings, while its legacy businesses stayed relatively weak. Gross margin
thus grew by 3.2ppt YoY during the year, from 29.2% in 2023 to 32.4% in 2024. Increasing
AI computing needs further increased power consumption and cooling requirements per
rack, boosting demand for high-density AI power and cooling products along with related

system-level solutions in 2025, which lifted gross margin by another 1.8ppt YoY to 34.3%
for the year.
The opex ratio was largely stable, fluctuating around 18-19% between 2020 and 2023. It
then increased to 19% in 2023 and 21.1% in 2024, mainly due to operating deleverage,
higher R&D costs, and ongoing capacity expansion. The ratio declined back to 19.1% in
2025 on the back of an enlarged revenue base, despite R&D-related investments
remaining elevated.

Looking ahead, we expect gross margin to continue expanding on the back of increasing
revenue contribution from AI-related power supplies. We thus pencil in a 2.0ppt gross
margin expansion to 36.2% in 2026, followed by further increases of 1.3ppt and 0.5ppt
YoY in 2027 and 2028, respectively, reaching 38.0% by 2028. On the opex ratio, we
expect Delta to continue benefiting from operating leverage in the coming years, and thus
estimate operating margin to improve more substantially than gross margin, expanding by

2.7ppt YoY in 2026 to 17.8%, followed by further expansions of 2.8ppt and 1.5ppt in 2027
and 2028, reaching 20.6% and 22.1%, respectively.

Net profit to grow at a 63% CAGR in 2025-28
Delta's operating profit grew sequentially throughout 2020-22, supported by gross
margin expansion and a lower opex ratio, from NT$31,426mn in 2020 to NT$41,439mn in
2022. It then saw a slight 1% YoY decline in 2023 due to higher operating expenses as the
company further stepped up its R&D investments, despite a 0.4ppt gross margin
expansion in 2023. Operating margin also edged down accordingly by 0.6ppt to 10.2%

during the year.
Operating profit returned to a growth trajectory from 2024 onward, growing 16% YoY in
2024 and 76% YoY in 2025, as AI-related power offerings started to scale up. Operating
margin expanded faster than gross margin, supported by operating leverage and
disciplined cost controls, increasing by 1.1ppt in 2024 to 11.3% and another 3.8ppt to 15.1%
in 2025.
With the tax rate staying largely stable at ~20% during 2020-23 before starting to edge

up in 2024 and reaching 23% in 2025, Delta's net profit growth was largely aligned with
its operating profit growth trajectory. 2023 was an exception, as net profit still managed
to grow 2% YoY despite a 1% YoY decline in operating profit, mainly due to higher FX
gains.
The net margin trend has been largely aligned with that of operating margin in recent
years. After dropping from 9.0% in 2020 to 8.5% in 2021, as lower gross margin was
offset by a lower opex ratio, net margin hovered around the 8.3-8.5% level during 2021-

24. Net margin was largely flat YoY in 2024 despite a 1.1ppt expansion in operating margin,
mainly due to lower non-operating income and a slightly higher tax rate. We then saw a
surge in 2025, with net margin expanding 2.5ppt YoY to 10.8%, benefiting from a larger
contribution from higher-margin AI power offerings.
Looking ahead, we forecast net profit to grow 72% YoY in 2026e, followed by 63% YoY
growth in 2027e and 56% YoY growth in 2028e, implying a 63% CAGR in 2025-28e,

assuming a stable tax rate of 22%. Net margin is likely to rise accordingly, from 10.8% in
2025 to 13.3% in 2026, followed by further expansion to 14.9% in 2027 and 15.8% in 2028.

ROE to rise alongside margin expansion
ROE rose gradually but steadily during 2020-22, from 14.3% to 14.5%, mainly driven by
improving asset turnover, offset by lower net margin. ROE edged down to 13.8% in 2023
and fell further to 12.6% in 2024 on the back of lower asset turnover, while net margin

and financial leverage remained at similar levels. It then rebounded to 18.5% in 2025,
supported by both improving asset turnover and net margin expansion, which was fueled
by an increasingly favorable product mix and operating leverage.
Looking ahead, we expect ROE to continue rising, reaching 25.9% in 2026, 32.7% in 2027,
and 37.8% in 2028, primarily driven by continued margin expansion and improving asset
turnover. Net margin is projected to expand from 10.8% in 2025 to 15.8% in 2028,

supported by gross margin improvement—from 34.3% to 38.0%—and a declining
operating expense ratio from 19.1% in 2025 to 15.9% in 2028.

Improving balance sheet and continuous free cash flow
generation
Delta has generated positive operating cash flow in recent years. Operating cash flow
declined in 2021, mainly due to higher inventory buildup amid supply chain disruptions
during the year. Free cash flow generation has also remained positive, while fluctuating
with capital expenditures over the years. Free cash flow was exceptionally low at NT
$5,292mn in 2021, as the company started to step up capex mainly for geographic capacity

diversification and greater production flexibility—adding new capacity in Thailand, India,
Tainan/Zhongli in Taiwan, and Wuhu, Wujiang, and Chongqing in Mainland China. Both
absolute capex and the capex-to-sales ratio have trended upward since 2022, as Delta
continued to add capacity for EV-related offerings and power and cooling solutions during
2023-24, with further acceleration in 2025 due to capacity tightness for AI power and
cooling offerings, especially in Thailand and the US.

Looking ahead, the company aims to continue adding capacity worldwide to fulfill
substantial demand for power and liquid-cooling turnkey solutions for large-scale data
centers operated by major hyperscalers beyond 2027. Capacity remains tight for AI power
and cooling offerings, so it continues to expand actively in China, Thailand, the US, and
Tainan. Management also plans to add mass-production lines in Taoyuan, Taiwan,
scheduled to be ready by 2028, in addition to the pilot-run lines in Tainan. Accordingly, we

now estimate capex to rise to NT$70bn in 2026 from ~NT$46bn in 2025 and stay at a
similar level in 2027 before edging down slightly to NT$60bn in 2028.
Delta's net cash position has been rising since 2021, with its net cash-to-equity ratio
improving from just 2% in 2021 to 40% in 2025. We expect this ratio to continue rising,
reaching 51% in 2026, 57% in 2027, and 63% in 2028, on strong free cash flow generation
despite a higher total debt level amid ongoing capacity expansion.

Company Background –  Delta
Company snapshot
Delta Electronics is a global provider of power and thermal management technologies,
with a focus on energy-efficient products and integrated solutions. Founded in 1971, the
company serves a broad range of applications spanning components, power and system
products, thermal management, mobility, industrial and building automation, data centers,
telecom, and energy infrastructure, across industries including automotive, healthcare,

data centers, and power grids. By combining its capabilities in power electronics, digital
technologies, and system integration, Delta aims to support customers in improving
operational efficiency and advancing smart manufacturing, smart cities, and more
sustainable infrastructure.
The company is headquartered in Taipei, Taiwan. Its global network consists of 293
operations, including 165 sales offices, 55 manufacturing sites, and 73 R&D centers,

supporting customers worldwide.

Product offerings
Delta's key offerings can be categorized into four key areas: Power Electronics,
Infrastructure, Mobility, and Automation.
1. Power Electronics
Delta operates in switching power supplies, thermal management, and passive

components, supplying products for ICT, cloud computing, EVs, and industrial and
commercial applications. The company frames its business around energy efficiency and

### Page 77

M
Asia Pacific Insight
Morgan Stanley Research 77
sustainability and now also offers grid-to-chip power and thermal management solutions
for data centers.
• Components: Includes essential passive and magnetic components used in portable
devices, AI cloud computing, automotive electronics, and IoT.
• Power and System: Contains power products and systems for AI, cloud computing,
IoT, client devices, and industrial, medical, lighting, e-mobility, and electrical-

appliance applications.
• Fans and Thermal Management: This mainly covers air and liquid cooling solutions
for AI cloud and edge computing, EVs, as well as industrial, commercial, and
residential sectors. Key products includes DC and EC fans, thermal modules, liquid
cooling cold plates, and  coolant distribution units, etc.

2. Infrastructure
Delta supplies ICT and energy infrastructure to support demand related to AI, smart cities,
and IoT. Its ICT infrastructure solutions target data centers and telecom networks, while

its energy infrastructure offerings integrate AIoT, power conversion, and microgrid control
across energy generation, storage, and management.
• ICT Infrastructure: It mainly provides infrastructure for AI computing and 5G

### Page 78

M
Asia Pacific Insight
78
communications under this category, ranging from telecom cell sites to data
centers. The portfolio includes power, air and liquid cooling, networking
equipment, and management software.
• Energy Infrastructure: The company offers renewable energy, AIoT-based energy
management, and power conversion technologies for low-carbon transformation.
Solutions include solar inverters, wind power converters, energy storage, EV
charging, and management platforms for smart grids. It also supplies

semiconductor high-voltage power supplies, automated testing instruments, and
variable-frequency drives for industrial processes.
• Display and Visualization: Delta's display technologies serve control rooms, public
information systems, projection mapping, and exhibition or event spaces. Offerings
range from large-scale and 8K projection systems to integrated video-wall
solutions and can be customized for mission-critical operations.

3. Mobility
Delta applies its power electronics and motor technologies to smart mobility, supplying

power systems, drive technologies, and integrated powertrain solutions. It works with

4. Automation
Delta provides automation solutions for industrial and building applications through
integrated software and hardware. Its industrial automation offerings support smart

production and operational efficiency, while its building automation solutions target
energy-efficient, secure, and sustainable spaces.
• Industrial Automation: This segment provides automation solutions across
industries by utilizing AI, big data, IoT, and digitized manufacturing models.
Relevant offerings support diverse production needs, including customized, small-
volume and high-variety production, as well as mass production.
• Building Automation: Delta's Building Automation Solutions use an IoT

architecture and integrate technologies from five brands: Amerlux lighting, Delta
Controls and LOYTEC IP-based building control, and March Networks and
VIVOTEK surveillance. The management platform enables operators to manage
energy use, occupant comfort, and sustainability goals through a single interface.

Monthly sales vs. share price
Delta's monthly sales show that seasonality has been an important factor over the years.
Following a substantial increase in monthly sales since 2024, traditional seasonal patterns
have become less pronounced as revenue from AI-related offerings has started to

outweigh them, while liquid-cooling-related offerings have added a new layer of monthly
sales volatility. The correlation between the company's monthly sales and share price
movements has remained high, at ~87% since 2023.
The company reported August monthly sales of NT$64,586mn (-4% MoM/+35% YoY). We
saw the Power Electronics segment decline sequentially from a high base, offset by
growth in Automation and slight growth in the Infrastructure segment.

Key management & shareholding structure
Delta's board currently consists of eight directors and five independent non-executive
directors. Its founder, Chung-Hua Cheng (Bruce Cheng), and his sons, Ping Cheng and An
Cheng, together owned a 7% stake as of March 2026. Excluding the founder and his
family, the management team held a stake of ~0.1% as of March 2026.
The FINI holding percentage as of July 14, 2026, was 63%, versus a previous peak of 78% in

February 2011 and a historical average (since 2010) of 69%.

Valuation Methodology – Lite-On
Our price target (base case scenario value) is NT$315, derived from 17x 2027-28e P/E:
We use price/earnings as our valuation methodology because we believe it appropriately
captures Lite-On's earnings growth potential. We believe our target P/E multiple of 17x is
justified by an earnings CAGR of 47% over 2025-28, implying a PEG of 0.4. This is lower
than the average PEG of 7.2 in 2022-25, when the stock was trading at an average P/E of

16x for a 2% earnings CAGR.
Bull case scenario value – NT$500: We apply a 27x P/E multiple to our average base case
estimate for 2027-28. This scenario assumes better-than-expected margin expansion,
driven by improved operating efficiency and accelerating production automation, while
new business revenue comes in earlier than expected. Growth in cloud computing and
automotive electronics is stronger than expected.
Bear case scenario value – NT$200: We apply an 11x P/E multiple to our average base

case estimate for 2027-28. This scenario assumes weaker-than-expected improvement in
operating efficiency, while pricing competition intensifies amid macro volatility;
automotive electronics take longer to ramp up.

Global Footprint – Lite-On
More capacity expansion to come
Lite-On's production footprint spans the globe, with 15 sites in total, although it remains
heavily weighted toward China. The company has been expanding aggressively, both to
meet strong demand for AI power supplies and to support its strategy of geographic risk
diversification and proximity to customers.
Looking at its expansion plans in recent years, the company has continued to demonstrate

its commitment to diversifying production away from China and increasing proximity to
customers. It completed its Thailand plant expansion and announced the second-phase
expansion of its Vietnam facility in 2021. It then established automotive electronics and
cloud power production lines in Dallas, Texas, in 2H22, along with the official launch of
the second phase of its Hai Phong plant in Vietnam, which focuses on networking and
consumer products. In 2023, it began expanding its second Kaohsiung plant and

announced additional capacity expansion in Vietnam. In 2024, it committed a further US
$120mn to establish a wholly owned subsidiary in Vietnam and began progressively
shifting production of high-end power products to that facility. In 2025, the company
aimed to double capacity as demand for AI server power supplies and battery backup units
(BBUs) surged, focusing primarily on capacity expansion in the US, Kaohsiung, and
Vietnam.
For 2026, the company expects capacity allocation of ~40% in China, 27% in Vietnam,

20% in Taiwan, and 10% in the US, with the remaining non-China footprint (i.e., Mexico,
Brazil, and Thailand) accounting for ~3% of total capacity. It has also announced
investments of US$919mn and US$149mn in its US and Vietnam subsidiaries, respectively,
to continue expanding power-related capacity, mainly for PSUs. The capex budget for
2026 has also been raised to ~NT$13bn, versus ~NT$7bn in 2025.
Exhibit 115:Lite-on's global footprint
Source: Lite-on.

Morgan Stanley Research 89
Financial Analysis –  Lite-On
29% revenue CAGR in 2025-28
Lite-On's revenue growth was relatively moderate during 2020-22, with revenue
expanding at a CAGR of 5% over the period. The company benefited from surging cloud
computing demand, which drove increasing demand for power supplies, along with rising
demand from EV/automotive electronics, although growth was offset by elevated
component costs and supply-chain disruptions during COVID-19. Revenue then fell by 14%

in 2023 and 8% in 2024 due to a post-pandemic demand pullback across the consumer
electronics market and PC/NB model transitions, coupled with softening demand from EV-
related businesses. The IT segment declined 20%/11% YoY, while the opto-electronics
segment declined 11%/9% YoY in 2023/2024, respectively.
Revenue rebounded in 2025, growing 21% YoY, as the company began to benefit from
surging demand for AI/cloud server power supplies. Contributions from ramping high-

voltage/AI power infrastructure products began to increase, coupled with a partial
recovery in the IT segment, mainly fueled by power adapter shipments for game consoles
and gaming PCs/NBs, as well as related power offerings for low-Earth-orbit applications.
Looking ahead, we estimate revenue to expand at a 29% CAGR in 2025-28e, driven by the
AI server power demand boom, while we remain mindful of weakness in consumer/IT
demand amid elevated component costs.

By product segment, IT-related products were the primary revenue driver in past years,
while 2025 marked a crossover point, with the contribution from the Cloud & AIoT
segment surpassing that of the IT segment, mainly supported by AI power-related
offerings. IT revenue contribution declined from 72% in 2020 to 38% in 2025, and we
estimate it will reach 17% by 2028. For the Cloud & AIoT segment, revenue contribution
grew substantially from 27% in 2021 (0% in 2020) to 45% in 2025; we estimate it will

account for 74% of total revenue by 2028, on the back of surging AI power-related
demand. For opto-electronics, we expect revenue contribution to decline from 17% in
2025 to 9% in 2028.

Continuing margin expansion
Lite-On's gross margin rose steadily over the years, from 17.4% in 2020 to 22.0% in 2023,
thanks to a continually improving product mix. Gross margin dipped to 21.6% in 2024,
mainly due to a smaller revenue base, while contributions from AI/cloud had yet to scale

and margins were weighed down by learning-curve costs at the new Vietnam site. It then
rose to 22.9% in 2025 as the AI server power business began to scale alongside rising BBU
(battery backup unit) shipments, resulting in a more favorable product mix.
The opex ratio was largely stable at 10.5-11.0% during 2020-22. It then increased to 12.2%
in 2023 and 12.1% in 2024, mainly due to operating deleverage and higher R&D costs. The
opex ratio edged up further to 12.8% in 2025, mainly due to ongoing investment in R&D to

support new business development in AI server power supplies, BBUs, liquid cooling, etc.,
along with a one-off tariff prepayment.
Looking ahead, we expect margins to continue expanding on a favorable product mix. We
now pencil in a 2.1ppt gross margin improvement in 2026 to 25.0%, followed by
expansions of 1.3ppt and 1.1ppt in 2027 and 2028, to 26.3% and 27.4%, respectively. For
the opex ratio, we expect it to decline sequentially as the revenue base expands.

Accordingly, we estimate operating margin to improve by 3.3ppt to 13.4% in 2026,
followed by expansions of 1.7ppt in both 2027 and 2028, reaching 15.1% and 16.8%.

Net profit to grow at a 47% CAGR in 2025-28
Operating profit grew steadily alongside gross margin expansion during 2020-22, from NT
$10,206mn to NT$15,073mn. It then fell 4% YoY in 2023 and a further 11% YoY in 2024
due to higher operating expenses, mainly from R&D costs, despite gross margin expansion
in 2023. Operating margin increased by 1.1ppt YoY to 9.8% in 2023 amid a smaller revenue
base, before declining by 0.3ppt YoY to 9.5% in 2024 on lower gross margin and an

elevated opex ratio.
Operating profit rebounded by 29% YoY to NT$16,738mn in 2025, supported by both an
expanding revenue base and strengthening gross margin. Operating margin also improved,
increasing by 0.6ppt YoY to 10.1%, as the recovery in gross margin was partly offset by
higher opex.
With the tax rate remaining relatively stable at 20-24%, Lite-On's net profit growth has
generally tracked its operating profit growth. An exception occurred in 2023, when net

profit grew 3% YoY despite a 4% decline in operating profit, mainly due to higher FX gains
during the year.
Net margin has broadly tracked operating margin in recent years. It reached a record high
of 9.8% in 2023 on improving gross margin, despite a higher opex ratio. It then declined to
8.7% in 2024 on gross margin erosion before rebounding to 9.1% in 2025, benefiting from
increasing demand for AI power supplies.
Looking ahead, we forecast net profit to grow 67% YoY in 2026e, followed by 44% YoY

growth in 2027e and 32% YoY growth in 2028e, implying a 47% CAGR in 2025-28e. Net
margin is likely to rise to 11.2% in 2026, 12.2% in 2027, and 13.4% in 2028.

ROE to rise
ROE movements were highly correlated with changes in net margin during 2020-22, as
asset turnover and financial leverage were largely stable over the period. ROE edged
down slightly in 2023 despite higher net margin, which increased from 8.2% to 9.8%, due

to lower asset turnover and financial leverage. ROE fell further to a trough of 13.1% in
2024 as net margin and asset turnover declined further. It then rebounded to 16.8% in
2025, primarily supported by net margin expansion, fueled by a favorable product mix,
along with an improvement in asset turnover back to the 80% level.
Looking ahead, we expect ROE to continue rising, reaching 26.4% in 2026, 35.3% in 2027,
and 42.4% in 2028, primarily driven by continued margin expansion and improving asset

turnover. Net margin is projected to increase from 9.1% in 2025 to 13.4% by 2028,
supported by gross margin improvement from 22.9% to 27.4% and a decline in the
operating expense ratio from 12.8% in 2025 to 10.5% in 2028.

Improving balance sheet and continuous free cash flow
generation
Lite-On has consistently generated positive operating cash flow in recent years, although
cash flow dropped notably in 2021, 2024, and 2025, mainly due to higher working-capital-
related cash outflows. The company has also consistently generated positive free cash
flow, with capital expenditures generally at the NT$3-4bn level over the years. An
exception occurred in 2025, when capital expenditure rose to NT$6.7bn, mainly for power-

related capacity expansion in Vietnam, Taiwan, and the US.
As the company has announced investments of US$919mn and US$149mn in its US and
Vietnam subsidiaries, respectively, alongside continued expansion of power-related
capacity, mainly for PSUs, we now estimate capex to rise to ~NT$13bn in 2026, versus ~NT
$7bn in 2025. Capex should then decline to NT$9bn in 2027 as investment levels
normalize.
Lite-On's net cash position has remained relatively healthy, with its net cash-to-equity

ratio improving from 65% in 2020 to 80% in 2023. It then declined to 75% in 2024 and
66% in 2025, as total debt increased by NT$7.7bn in 2024, while cash on hand declined in
2025, likely due to increasing capital investment.
Looking ahead, we assume the total debt position will remain stable despite ongoing
capex investment. The net cash position should continue to grow sequentially on the back
of positive free cash flow generation.

Lite-On's average capex-to-sales ratio was around 2.3-2.4% during 2021-24, then rose to
4.1% in 2025 as the company began aggressively expanding global capacity, despite a

larger revenue base during the year. Looking ahead, we project the capex-to-sales ratio to
rise to 5.9% in 2026 amid continued production-site development, before declining to 3.0-
3.3% in 2027-28 as investments normalize.

Lite-On's cash conversion cycle (CCC) fluctuated within a relatively narrow band of
roughly 35-44 days between 2020 and 2025, reflecting generally stable but modestly
shifting working-capital efficiency. CCC was at a relatively low level of 35 days in 2020,

supported by lower inventory days and higher AP days, before rising steadily to 44 days in
2022 as both inventory days (78) and AR days (85) increased, while AP days were slightly
lower (119). From 2023 onward, the cycle improved meaningfully, falling to 36 days as AP
days extended sharply from 119 in 2022 to 135. This improvement partially reversed in
2024, with CCC ticking up to 39 days as inventory and AR days both increased to 89 and
92 days, respectively, even as AP days extended further to 143. CCC then eased again to 37

days in 2025 as AP days moderated slightly to 138, alongside stable inventory and
receivables levels.
Looking ahead, we expect the overall cash conversion cycle to edge up slightly from 37
days in 2025 to 40 days in 2028, as increasing AR days and lower AP days are partially
offset by improving inventory days.

Company Background –  Lite-On
Company snapshot
Founded in 1975, Lite-On Technology is a Taiwan-based manufacturer of optoelectronic
components, power supplies, and information technology products. The company
produced Taiwan's first LEDs and became the first technology company listed on the
Taiwan Stock Exchange in 1983. It mainly operates through four business segments:
Information Products (power supplies, servers, networking equipment, keyboards, and

chassis), Optoelectronics (LED packaging, LED lighting, automotive electronic
components, and camera modules), Storage (storage components), and other supporting
businesses. Additional product lines include energy management solutions, such as data
center power supply systems and cloud server chassis; networking and surveillance
products; and automotive electronics, including ADAS and EV charging solutions.
Lite-On is headquartered in Taipei, Taiwan. The company operates manufacturing sites,

R&D centers, and sales offices across Asia (Taiwan, China, Japan, and Singapore), the US,
and Europe (the UK, the Netherlands, and Germany).

Product offerings
Lite-On's key offerings can be categorized into six key areas: Optoelectronic
Semiconductors, Energy Management, Consumer Electronics, Networking and
Surveillance, Automotive Electronics, and Roadway Intellimation System.
1. Optoelectronic semiconductors
Its product portfolio spans a broad technological range, encompassing illumination and
sensing applications, low- to high-power devices, standard and custom-made solutions,
and components ranging from LEDs to lasers and from visible light to infrared and

ultraviolet spectra.
Exhibit 144:Lite-on: Offerings for optoelectronic semiconductors

Source: Company data, Morgan Stanley Research
2. Energy management

The product range serves the communications, industrial automation, smart home,
medical, office, and automotive electronics industries. It offers a comprehensive suite of
platinum- to titanium-efficiency-grade power supplies (CRPS, GPU, and SLIM series)
suitable for both standard and AI servers, supporting AC or DC input with high power
density, high reliability, and modular scalability. Power-shelf and battery-backup-unit
(BBU) solutions, along with rack-cooling and liquid-cooling technologies for data centers,

cloud infrastructure, and AI/machine-learning platforms, are also included in this segment.

Lite-On is able to leverage its core competencies in optoelectronics and power

6. Roadway Intellimation System

This segment is mainly operated under the LEOTEK brand, a subsidiary of the LITEON
Technology Group with dual headquarters in Silicon Valley and Taipei. It has served road
infrastructure across more than 30 countries—including North America, Europe, the
Middle East, and the Asia-Pacific region—since 1992. The segment comprises intelligent
roadway lighting, traffic signal systems, and AI-driven predictive maintenance solutions, all
developed to support smarter and more efficient road infrastructure.

we see a high correlation of ~76% between the company's monthly sales and its share
price movements since 2023.

Key management & shareholding structure
Lite-On's board currently consists of five directors and four independent non-executive
directors.
Its founder, Raymond Soong, and current Chairman, Tom Soong, together owned a stake
of ~5% as of June 2026. Excluding the founder and Chairman, the management team

owned a stake of ~0.1% as of June 2026.
The FINI holding percentage as of July 7, 2026, was 41%, versus a previous peak of 62% in
April 2017 and a historical average (since 2010) of 46%.

ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan

Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone.  In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice.  An investor's decision

Analyst Stock Ratings
Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next
12-18 months.
Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over

the next 12-18 months.
Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage
universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.
Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next

12-18 months.
Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.
Analyst Industry Views
Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated
below.
In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.
Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia -

股價相對大盤走勢

穩懋 (3105 TT)  Buy

PIN 等光通訊訂單效應持續推升業績向上，評等為 Buy。  
投資建議 ：穩懋 08/2026 營收 19.37 億元， MoM+2.40% ，符合
群益先前預估， 3Q26 營收動能來自 optical 、 infrastructure ，預
估 09/2026 營收仍將較 08/2026 增加。就評價而言，穩懋受惠於
PIN 等光通訊新訂單自 06/2026 開始量產，預估出貨量 4Q26 將
持續放大，且後續成長空間可期，為 4Q26 、2027 年重要營收動
能，故群益維持對穩懋 Buy 的投資建議。  
2Q26 稅後淨利 QoQ+83.22% ： 2Q26 營收 52.57 億元，
QoQ+14.54% ，動能來自 cellular 、infrastructure 、optical 等產品，
此外，雖然 optical 新產品量產初期良率較低，不過，由於 2Q26
產品結構優於 1Q26 、產能利用率提高，毛利率提高至 28.24% ，
2Q26 營業利益 QoQ+72.14% ，加上認列 1.44 億元匯兌利益、
2.70 億元投資利益， 2Q26 稅後淨利增加至 9.77 億元，
QoQ+83.22% 。  
就 2Q26 的產品結構而言， cellular 產品營收 QoQ+18.00% ，原
因在於手機業者需求隨逐漸進入旺季而增溫； infrastructure 產
品營收 QoQ+14.53% ，動能來自 AI data center 、低軌道衛星；  
optical 產品 2Q26 重要動能有二， (1) 新款 iPhone 相關 VCSEL
訂單數量增加， (2)PIN 等光通訊新訂單自 06/2026 開始量產。  
PIN 等光通訊新訂單出貨量放大為後續重要營收動能： 展望
3Q26 ， optical 產品受惠於 PIN 等光通訊新訂單出貨量放大，營
收成長性居各產品之冠，預估將帶動 others 產品營收
QoQ+35.97% ，且後續成長空間仍可期；其次， infrastructure 產
品出貨動能來自低軌道衛星標案陸續出貨，預估相關營收
QoQ+20.45% ，由於全球各地低軌道衛星標案隨應用領域拓展而
有機會持續釋出，後續營收前景亦仍佳。  
至於 cellular 、 Wi-Fi 產品出貨成長性雖受到近期記憶體缺料等
因素影響整體手機產業景氣而存在不確定性，但現階段預估營
收均與 2Q26 實績相去有限。

### Page 2

個股報告  
                                        2026 年 10 月 5 日

2 群益投顧 
  企業 ESG 資訊揭露：  
就企業溫室氣體排放量減量之預算與計畫而言，穩懋持續透過各項節能措
施提升廠內能源使用效率，以減少用電度數，且新設產線全面採用最佳可行
技術，實際措施包括 PFCs 尾氣處理設備全面採用去除效率達 90% 以上之型
式 (IPCC 公告指定型式 )、逐步汰換既設產線之 PFCs 尾氣處理設備、評估製
程氣體減量使用或取代高潛勢溫室氣體使用、採取工程改善或設備更換以
降低用電量、評估新建廠房設置廠內再生能源等。  
再就企業產品或服務帶給客戶或消費者之減碳效果而言，穩懋致力於新技
術的開發，持續推出高效能的技術平台，以生產更具能源效益和節能的半導
體通訊產業產品，因終端產品應用逐漸擴及手機、無線網路、雷達、衛星等
各個通訊領域，有助於全球能源節省和減少用電消耗。此外，藉由穩懋多功
能的晶片整合、製程線寬的縮減以及製程能力的提升，終端產品之尺寸、單
位晶片使用量均持續減少，亦得以為守護地球環境盡一份心力。

個股報告  
                                        2026 年 10 月 5 日

6 群益投顧 
  【投資評等說明】  
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首次評等潛在上漲空間 ≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy) 5% ≤首次評等潛在上漲空間 <15%

中立 (Neutral)  
無法由基本面給予投資評等  
預期近期股價將處於盤整  
建議降低持股

【投資評等調整機制】  
  投資評等係「首次」給予特定個股投資評等時，其前一日收盤價相較 3 個月
目標價之潛在上漲空間計算而得。個股投資評等分為四個等級，定義如上。
爾後的投資評等係依循「首次評等」，直到停止推薦。   
「強力買進」、「買進」及「區間操作」均有 upside 目標價。差別僅在於，
首次評等時潛在報酬率不同。「中立」則無目標價。  
一旦我們給予特定個股「強力買進」 、 「買進」或「區間操作」之投資評等，
就是責任的開始，爾後將透過各式研究報告作定期性、持續性追蹤基本面及
股價變化，直到停止推薦。  
停止推薦情境：  
1.  達目標價。  
2.  雖未達原訂目標價，但檢視基本面、訊息面、籌碼面等多方訊息，研
判股價上漲空間已然有限，將適時出具降評報告。  
3.  推薦後股價不漲反跌。

【免責聲明】  
  本研究報告僅提供予特定人之客戶作為參考資料「非經同意不得轉載」 。我
們並不確保此資訊的完整性與正確性，投資人應了解，報告中有關未來預測
之陳述可能不會實現，因而不應被依賴。而且此報告並非根據特定投資目的
或依預定對象之財務狀況所撰寫出來的，因此，此研究報告的目的，既非對
投資人於買賣證券、選擇權、期貨或其他證券相關之衍生性商品提供詢價服
務，亦非作為進行交易的要約。投資人應注意到相關證券之價值及收益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。
